## What the research changed

Project A — Lecture ASR. The anomaly I pitched held up and got sharper: Whisper large-v3 scores 19.0% WER on Indian accents against Whisper small's 17.6% — and the largest model is also the *least equitable* across accent groups. Meanwhile Canary-Qwen 2.5B leads the Open ASR Leaderboard at 5.63% while training on an order of magnitude less data than Whisper. Both point the same way: for an under-represented accent, targeted in-domain data beats scale. That's now the project's thesis, and base-model choice became an experiment (task 2.6) rather than an assumption.

I also found the closest precedent — an Interspeech 2025 paper adapting Whisper to Hindi–English code-mix reporting 14% and 28.1% relative error reductions. Your S1 target of ≥20% is now evidence-based rather than invented. It's not on arXiv, so I pulled it from the ISCA archive.

Project B — Respiratory sounds. Mostly confirmed what I had, with one correction worth flagging: the 57.3% ICBHI figure circulating in some papers is a *historical* baseline, not current state of the art. The real bar is 64.48% (Lung-SRAD) against a 50.0% trivial floor. The spec says so explicitly so you don't accidentally claim a false win.

Project C — Air quality. Here I have to correct something I told you in the pitch. I said persistence usually beats deep learning for PM2.5. That was overstated — the evidence actually shows deep models usually do win, with one Beijing study as a counterexample. But digging further turned up something better: two published Delhi PM2.5 studies report R² = 0.995 and R² = 0.499. That gap is too large to be method differences — one is almost certainly predicting PM2.5 from *concurrent* pollutants rather than forecasting from the past. So the headline experiment is now Phase 6: run the identical model under forecasting vs nowcasting conditions and measure how much leakage buys. That's a sharper project than the one I originally pitched.

## Two notes

The paper titles in _metadata.json are slightly messy — extracted from PDF page 1, so some picked up author names or "Published as a conference paper at ICLR 2023". The PDFs themselves are correct and verified; SUMMARIES.md has the clean titles.

TIE (project A) needs an access request — ~750 GB behind a form. Do it day 1. But you don't need 8,740 hours for LoRA, and NPTEL2020 plus Svarah download directly, so nothing blocks on it.

Each SUMMARIES.md ends with a diagram of how its papers relate, and a suggested reading order. For C, read the forecast-evaluation paper before writing any code — it's the one that stops you fooling yourself.

Measured on your actual machine rather than guessing. Here's the honest answer.

## Your laptop

i7-1260P, 12 cores, 16 GB DDR4-3200. Two numbers decide everything:

| Measured | Value | What it limits |

|---|---|---|

| Memory bandwidth (multi-threaded) | 32.5 GB/s | Token generation speed |

| fp32 matrix throughput | 165 GFLOP/s | Image encoding / prefill |

Decoding is bandwidth-bound — every generated token reads the whole model from RAM. So model size maps almost directly to speed:

| Model | Quantised | Decode ceiling | Page latency |

|---|---|---|---|

| Qwen2.5-VL-3B | ~2.2 GB | ~16 tok/s | ~2–3 min |

| 0.9 B document VLM | ~0.6 GB | ~54 tok/s | ~25–50 s |

| Compact det+rec (~35 M) | ~40 MB | — | ~1–3 s |

## So: yes, but not the way I originally specified it

A fine-tuned 3B VLM will run on your laptop — at roughly two minutes a page. That's fine for a 20-page document overnight. It's useless for an archive, and it's not what you asked for.

I've restructured the project around this. The accuracy model and the deployable model don't have to be the same model:

  • Track A — fine-tune the VLM on Kaggle GPU. This is the research result that beats 75.2. It's allowed to be slow.
  • Track B — use that model to pseudo-label a large pile of unlabelled Indic scans, then train a compact student on those labels. This is the thing that runs on your laptop in seconds.

That's not an invention — it's exactly what Distil-Whisper does for speech, and I already have that paper in the lecture-ASR folder. It works because OCR is narrow: a 3B VLM spends most of its capacity on general visual reasoning it never uses for transcription.

New success criteria: S3 = ≤10 s/page on your CPU, S4 = student stays within 5 chrF++ of the teacher.

## Two things I found that change the plan

A 0.9B document VLM exists. PaddleOCR-VL is purpose-built for multilingual document parsing at 0.9B — 3–5× faster than Qwen-3B on your machine and potentially a better base model. I've added it as task 0.7: evaluate its Devanagari support before committing, because "multilingual" doesn't always include Indic scripts. If it works, it's better on both axes.

Don't count on your Iris Xe. I checked, expecting it to be a free speedup. It isn't — the guidance for Iris Xe specifically is that the iGPU gives no benefit over the CPU for this workload. Budget CPU only. The real optimisation is OpenVINO INT8 quantisation, which gives a genuine 2–3× on Intel CPUs.

## What I'd do first

Task 0.8: pull down a 3B VLM, run one Devanagari page through it on your CPU, and time it. That single measurement tells you whether Track B is a nice-to-have or the whole point — and it takes twenty minutes.

Files updated: proposal.md, specs.md, tasks.md (new Phase 4b), plus two PaddleOCR-VL papers downloaded.

# PP-OCRv6: Architecture & Performance Analysis

## Core Architecture

**Detection Pipeline:**
The system uses LCNetV4 backbone feeding into RepLKFPN (Reparameterizable Large-Kernel Feature Pyramid Network) and a DB head. The detection neck employs "dilated depthwise branches (5×5 with dilation 1, 3×3 with dilation 2, and 3×3 with dilation 3)" that merge into a single 7×7 convolution at inference via structural reparameterization.

**Recognition Pipeline:**
Text images pass through the LCNetV4 backbone with asymmetric stride (2,1) to preserve width, then through EncoderWithLightSVTR neck (for medium/small models). The decoder combines CTC for inference with NRTR for training-time regularization.

## Model Tiers

Three configurations spanning parameter counts:
- **Tiny**: 1.5M end-to-end (0.43M detection, 1.1M recognition)
- **Small**: 7.7M end-to-end (2.48M detection, 5.2M recognition)  
- **Medium**: 34.5M end-to-end (15.5M detection, 19M recognition)

## Training Techniques

**Knowledge Distillation**: The tiny recognition model uses "KL-divergence loss that aligns the student's CTC output probability distribution with the teacher's," enabling vocabulary-matched logit alignment.

… [2,493 more characters]

PP-OCRv6 is decisive for Plan A — 34.5M params beating Qwen3-VL-235B on OCR, and it supports 50 languages but not Indic. Now researching Plan B's side.

Both plans written. The research changed my view of which one is the "real" project.

## The finding that reframed everything

PP-OCRv6 (2026) — "From 1.5M to 34.5M Parameters, Surpassing Billion-Scale VLMs on OCR Tasks":

| | PP-OCRv6 medium (34.5 M) | Billion-scale VLM |

|---|---|---|

| Recognition accuracy | 83.2% | Qwen3-VL-235B: 74.9% |

| Detection Hmean | 86.2% | Gemini-3.1-Pro: 46.8% |

| Hallucination-free | 93.2% | Kimi-K2.6: 85.0% |

6,800× fewer parameters, higher score. And SVTRv2 (ICCV 2025) is literally titled *"CTC Beats Encoder-Decoder Models in Scene Text Recognition."*

So the laptop model is not the consolation prize. It may win outright.

And PP-OCRv6 supports 50 languages — Chinese, Japanese, English, 46 Latin-script. No Indic. The architecture that beats billion-parameter VLMs has never been trained for Devanagari. That's the gap both plans attack from opposite ends.

## [Plan A — Local CPU](indic-document-ocr/plan-A-local-cpu.md)

Two-stage pipeline: LCNetV4 + RepLKFPN + DB detection → LCNetV4 + LightSVTR + CTC recognition. Tiers at 1.5M / 7.7M / 34.5M.

Why CTC is the whole point. CTC emits an entire line in *one forward pass*. Autoregressive decoding emits one token at a time, and on your laptop that's bandwidth-bound at 32.5 GB/s. For a page of ~800 tokens that's roughly two orders of magnitude.

Latency, from PP-OCRv6's Xeon+OpenVINO figures adjusted for your i7-1260P: ~0.4–0.8 s (tiny), ~3–5 s (medium). Plus 5.2× from OpenVINO INT8.

The hard part isn't the architecture — it's the label unit. The CTC output layer is vocabulary-wide, and Devanagari conjuncts run to hundreds of composite glyphs. PP-OCRv6's tiny tier drops Japanese purely to control output-layer size. Full conjuncts vs codepoints vs grapheme clusters is Phase A1, before any training.

## [Plan B — Kaggle GPU](indic-document-ocr/plan-B-kaggle-gpu.md)

Base model is now an evaluation, not an assumption — the field moved past Qwen. Candidates: dots.ocr (1.7B, 100+ languages, beats models 20× its size), PaddleOCR-VL (0.9B), GOT-OCR 2.0 (580M), DeepSeek-OCR (3B, compresses vision tokens 7–20×), MinerU2.5 (decoupled layout→recognition).

Those last two are the SOTA answers to your resolution/VRAM problem specifically.

Fine-tuning follows a direct precedent I found: "Finetuning VLMs as OCR Systems for Low-Resource Languages: A Case Study of Manchu" — same problem shape, r=64, α=64, adapters on both vision and language components, synthetic data transferring to real historical scans.

## The head-to-head is the actual research question

Phase 5.2: does the 35M model beat the 3B one on Indic? PP-OCRv6 says it should. Either answer is a result worth reporting, and you'll have measured it rather than assumed it.

## One gap

papers/SUMMARIES.md still covers only the original 8 papers — I added 14 more (PP-OCRv6, SVTRv2, PARSeq, GOT-OCR2, MinerU2.5, DeepSeek-OCR, Manchu, OmniDocBench, OCRBench v2, PaddleOCR-VL ×2, PaddleOCR 3.0, PP-OCRv5, SVTRv2X-adjacent). All 22 PDFs are downloaded and title-verified; the summaries for the new ones aren't written yet.

Want me to write those next, or start on Phase 0/1 — getting the benchmark and reproducing the 58.3 and 75.2 baselines?

# UniLipi: Unified Multi-Script Historical OCR

## Script Coverage
UniLipi operates across 13 Indic scripts spanning five regional traditions: North/Central Indian (Devanagari, Sharada, Modi), Western Indian (Gurmukhi, Jaini), Eastern Indian (Bengali, Odia), Southern Indian (Grantha, Telugu, Malayalam, Kannada), and Himalayan/Buddhist (Newar, Siddham).

## Unified Modeling Approach
Rather than maintaining separate OCR systems per script, UniLipi employs joint training across all scripts within a single framework. The model learns shared visual representations while remaining sensitive to script-specific characteristics. This approach leverages the observation that many Indic manuscripts encode the same underlying language—notably Sanskrit—across diverse scripts.

## Output Representation
The system uses Roman WX transliteration as its unified decoding target. This representation provides "a deterministic, lossless mapping" that preserves orthographic units while enabling a single shared vocabulary. Predictions are reversible back to native Unicode scripts without ambiguity.

## Architecture
UniLipi combines CNN-Transformer components with multi-task heads. A ResNet backbone extract
… [987 more characters]

You were right, and pushing on it surfaced the single most important technical decision in the project.

## Scope is now 12 languages

Assamese · Bengali · Odia · Marathi · Hindi · Kannada · Urdu · Telugu · Malayalam · Tamil · Gujarati · Punjabi — matching IndicSTR12 (IIIT-CVIT), the largest real multi-script Indic benchmark, with ≥1000 real word images per language. Downloaded.

Devanagari stays *primary* only because it's the one with a published real-scan leaderboard to measure against (the 75.2 / 86.3 numbers). Not because the others are optional.

## The problem you exposed — and the fix

Going multi-script made Plan A's hardest problem much worse. The CTC output layer is as wide as the vocabulary, and 12 Indic scripts with their conjuncts run to thousands of glyphs. PP-OCRv6's tiny tier drops Japanese purely to control that width. Twelve Indic scripts is a far bigger ask.

The fix: don't output native script at all.

Devanagari / Tamil / Telugu / Bengali / …
        ↓  deterministic, lossless
   Roman WX  — ~60 shared symbols  ← the model predicts this
        ↓  fully reversible
   native Unicode

WX notation is an ASCII transliteration from IIT Kanpur where every consonant and vowel has one Roman mapping, forming a prefix code. The output layer collapses from thousands of glyphs to about sixty symbols, all 12 scripts share one vocabulary so visual features transfer between them, and nothing is lost.

UniLipi (2026) validates this across 13 Indic scripts — 6.9% CER overall, and the unified model *beats* script-specific training (Sharada: 5.2% unified vs 6.9% mono-script).

So the thing that looked like it would break multi-script is what makes it work.

## And it created a real research question

The literature genuinely disagrees:

| Position | Evidence |

|---|---|

| Unified wins | Shared vocabulary beats script-specific: 6.9 vs 14.1 CER |

| Per-script wins | Single decoders "scale poorly due to inflated vocabulary and pronounced inter-script confusions" |

The per-script camp's argument rests on *inflated vocabulary* — which WX removes. So Phase A1b runs all three arms (Unified / Multiplexed / Per-script) and settles it for printed Indic documents. That's a contribution, not just engineering.

## Two honest caveats now in the spec

UniLipi's evidence is weaker for us than it looks. Its manuscripts often encode the *same* language (Sanskrit) in different scripts — unusually favourable for cross-script sharing. We have genuinely different languages. That's why it's an experiment, not an assumption.

Urdu is handled separately. It's Perso-Arabic, right-to-left and cursive Nastaliq — it breaks left-to-right CTC assumptions. Forcing it into the shared model untested would be a mistake.

I've also added an expected pattern to check against: Tamil should lead (few conjuncts), Malayalam/Telugu/Kannada should lag (heavy conjuncts, stacked rounded forms). If that pattern doesn't appear, something's wrong with the data.

Per-script training-data volume gets reported alongside per-script accuracy — otherwise you can't tell a modelling failure from a data shortage, and the real data is heavily skewed toward Devanagari.

### 2.3 Splits



- **The benchmark's real scans are the test set and are never trained on.**

- Training data comes from separate corpora plus synthetic generation.

- Split training data by **document source / print style**, not by page â€” pages from the same book share

  fonts, paper and scanner settings.

- Hold out at least one entirely unseen document source for validation.

▸ PowerShell: Read plan A section 4.1
### 4.1 The label-unit decision â­



The CTC output layer is as wide as the vocabulary, so how we define an output symbol is the first decision.

**Measured on our downloaded data** (IndicSTR12 crops, 2,160 word images):



| Label unit | Vocabulary | Advantage | Cost |

|---|---|---|---|

| **Unicode codepoints** | **143** (measured) | Small layer, no extra machinery | Model must assemble conjuncts itself; CTC aligns to units with no separate visual extent |

| **Conjuncts / grapheme clusters** | **1000+** (Kannada *vattakshara* alone) | One symbol per visual unit â€” cleanest CTC alignment | Large output layer, long tail of rare glyphs |

| **Roman WX transliteration** | **~60**, shared across both scripts | Smallest layer; Hindi and Kannada share one vocabulary; reversible | Adds a transliteration step both ways |



**WX notation** is a deterministic, lossless ASCII transliteration from IIT Kanpur; every consonant and vowel

maps to one Roman form, and conversion back to native Unicode is unambiguous.



> **A correction to be honest about.** The "thousands of glyphs" framing applies to *conjunct-level*

> labelling. At codepoint level, our two scripts need only **143** symbo
… [535 more characters]

## Result

scripts/qc_data.py → 0 failures. All 30,947 images decode, every one is labelled, no label is empty or contains a character outside the charset.

| Split | Images | Devanagari | Kannada |

|---|---|---|---|

| train | 21,315 | 17,653 | 3,662 |

| val | 2,601 | 2,085 | 516 |

| test | 6,953 | 6,141 | 812 |

| total | 30,869 | 25,879 | 4,990 |

Plus 95,430 handwritten Devanagari images (separate manifest — different domain, shouldn't be mixed into printed training by default) and 800,042 Wikipedia lines for synthetic rendering.

## Two things the check caught that would have cost you weeks

The renderer couldn't render Indic text. Pillow only shapes complex scripts when built against libraqm, and the Windows wheels aren't. Unshaped, the i-matra ि stays *after* its consonant instead of before it, and conjuncts never form — क्+त draws as क, a visible virama, then त. It looks like plausible Devanagari to anyone who doesn't read the script, so it survives review and trains the model on text no real document contains. [indic_render.py](scripts/indic_render.py) replaces it with HarfBuzz+FreeType; verified visually and by self-test.

CTC geometry decides the label unit — this answers the open question in Plan A §4.1 with measurement rather than preference. A matra adds a codepoint but no width, so narrow words carry more characters than there are timesteps, returning infinite loss. At h=32 /4, 1.58% of samples are infeasible. Grapheme clusters fix that but blow the vocabulary from 151 to 1,954 classes for Devanagari — wrong trade for a CPU model. Settled: codepoint labels, height 48, stride /4 → 247 classes, 0.39% infeasible, residual 78 dropped from train/val and kept in test.

## What was actually wrong with the data

Very little. 2 images appeared in both train and test; 22 duplicates; one blank crop carrying two contradictory labels (एवं and एक — the image has no text at all). 20 labels had look-alike codepoints from neighbouring Indic blocks — a Gurmukhi bindi where the Devanagari one belongs — repaired by offset mapping.

The one substantive find: 3.3% of labels contained zero-width joiners, and they're noise, not orthography — का is written 8 different ways including <ZWJ><ZWNJ><ZWJ><ZWJ>का. Stripped.

I deliberately kept the 0.21% of very low-contrast crops. I checked them: they're night-time signage with text genuinely present and correctly labelled. Deleting hard examples to make a dataset look clean is how a model learns to fail in the field.

## Two caveats that affect how you report results

  • 63.7% of Devanagari test words also appear in training. The images differ, but BSTD is scene text and city/bank names recur — so BSTD test measures recognition, not generalisation. Further reason the stress-test benchmark stays the primary target.
  • The real data is word crops, not document lines (median 5–6 chars). It trains glyph shapes under real degradation; layout has to come from synthetic generation.

## Still open before synthetic generation

Only 3 fonts per script — a model trained on three typefaces overfits their letterforms. Target 15+ each (now task 0.4b). The Noto Indic fonts also carry no Latin glyphs, so the renderer needs a Latin fallback for mixed-script lines.

Full write-up in [reports/DATA_QC.md](reports/DATA_QC.md); [specs.md](specs.md) §2.3–2.6, [plan-A-local-cpu.md](plan-A-local-cpu.md) §4.1 and [tasks.md](tasks.md) Phase 0 are updated to match.

is the data good for training or we should clean it more?

also, if the data is good, start with the project.

## Quick start



Run any engine on a folder of images and score it against ground truth:



```bash

# score EasyOCR on the clean synthetic split

python scripts/runner.py --model easyocr --imgdir images/clean --tag clean



# score a frontier API model on the real scans (keys in ~/.ocr_keys)

python scripts/runner.py --model gemini --imgdir real/images --gt real/gt.json --tag real

```



API keys (closed models) are read from `~/.ocr_keys`:



```

GEMINI_API_KEY=...

ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=...

OPENAI_API_KEY=...

MISTRAL_API_KEY=...

```



## Reproduce



> [!TIP]

> Run all commands from the repo root. Local models need a CUDA GPU (a single 24 GB A10G suffices — one model resident at a time). Three uv venvs are used to satisfy conflicting `transformers` pins: EasyOCR (4.40), Qwen/Unlimited/Qwen3-VL/olmOCR + APIs (4.57.1), DeepSeek-OCR (4.46.3).



```bash

# 1. build the benchmark

python scripts/render.py -n 100          # synthetic, 4 conditions

python scripts/make_real.py              # 300 real printed scans (from HF)



# 2. run engines (per model x condition)

python scripts/runner.py --model <m> --img

▸ PowerShell: Inspect benchmark scoring code
#!/usr/bin/env python3

"""Unified OCR runner. Run ONE engine over ONE image dir, score vs gt.json,

append to out/results_matrix.json under key '<model>@<tag>'.

Engine imports are lazy so each runs in its own venv."""

import argparse, glob, json, os, re, tempfile, time, unicodedata



ROOT = os.path.expanduser("~/ocr-bench")

OUT = os.path.join(ROOT, "out")



def nfc(s): return unicodedata.normalize("NFC", s).strip()



def clean(s):

    s = re.sub(r"<\|.*?\|>", " ", s)

    s = re.sub(r"\[\s*\d+\s*(,\s*\d+\s*){1,3}\]", " ", s)

    s = re.sub(r"</?(ref|det)>", " ", s)

    s = s.replace("<page>", " ")

    return re.sub(r"\s+", " ", s).strip()



def score(refs, hyps):

    import jiwer, sacrebleu

    refs = [nfc(x) for x in refs]; hyps = [nfc(x) for x in hyps]

    return dict(

        CER=round(jiwer.cer(refs, hyps) * 100, 2),

        WER=round(jiwer.wer(refs, hyps) * 100, 2),

        chrFpp=round(sacrebleu.corpus_chrf(hyps, [refs], word_order=2).score, 2),

    )



def run_easyocr(paths):

    import easyocr

    r = easyocr.Reader(["hi", "en"], gpu=True)

    return [" ".join(r.readtext(p, detail=0, paragraph=True)).strip() for p in paths]

The data is good — I'd stop cleaning. So I started the project, and Phase 1 passed its gate.

## Data verdict: ready

Nothing left worth fixing. The defect rate after cleaning is ~0.1%, and the remaining warnings are all real-world properties I checked individually and decided to keep (repeated common words on different signboards, dark night-time signage). Further cleaning would start deleting hard examples, which makes the dataset look better and the model worse.

The one genuine gap isn't cleanliness, it's fonts — 3 per script, needed before synthetic generation.

## Phase 1: gate passed

Built the 300-image eval set and an evaluation harness using the benchmark's *own* scoring code, then reproduced their EasyOCR baseline on your CPU:

| Metric | Published | Ours | Δ |

|---|---|---|---|

| chrF++ | 58.3 | 57.89 | −0.41 |

| median CER | 16.7 | 16.67 | −0.03 |

| mean CER | 34.3 | 34.30 | 0.00 |

| catastrophic % | 26.3 | 27.3 | +1.0 |

Four independent statistics within a point — our numbers are comparable to their table. 0.3 s/image, CPU only.

## Three findings that changed the plan

The benchmark isn't what the proposal assumed. It's 300 *word* scans of Sanskrit type — median 1 word per image, clean and crisp, not degraded documents. Their README flags that the word-level format disadvantages page-oriented models. Your word-crop training data matches that granularity, so the task-shape gap I flagged earlier is smaller than I said.

EasyOCR's main failure is detection, not reading. 21.3% of images return *nothing* — median width 60px vs 96px for the rest; the detector won't fire on narrow crops. Excluding those, chrF++ goes 57.9 → 64.4. Plan A is recognition-only on pre-cropped words, so it never runs that detector. That's a real advantage here — and partly an artifact of the benchmark's format, so it has to be reported that way, not banked as recognition superiority.

I had the conjunct effect backwards. Raw numbers said conjunct-heavy words were *easier*. That was a length confound — longer words are wider, wider images get detected. Controlling for length reverses it: within 6–10 characters, 0 conjuncts → 20.6% CER, 1+ conjuncts → 37.5%.

## The domain gap, measured

The virama count per word measures conjunct density:

| Corpus | Viramas/word |

|---|---|

| Benchmark (Sanskrit) | 1.54 |

| Your Hindi training data | 0.39 |

| Hindi Wikipedia | 0.28 |

The benchmark is ~4× denser than your Hindi data, and Hindi Wikipedia — the planned synthetic source — is the *worst* match. So I pulled in-domain Sanskrit: 2,513 train / 279 val images from the benchmark's own source, excluding both the 300 eval images *and* the 245 whose label text appears in the eval set. Verified zero label overlap.

One caveat I want to be explicit about: training on the benchmark's source is an advantage the published zero-shot systems didn't have. Results need reporting as a pair — with and without that corpus — or the comparison is false. That's written into [reports/BASELINE.md](reports/BASELINE.md) §6.

## Next

Phase 2 is synthetic data, which needs the font expansion first (15+ per script) and Sanskrit text rather than just Hindi Wikipedia. Want me to carry on into that, or run the Qwen3-VL-8B baseline first? That one needs your Kaggle account — 8B won't fit comfortably on the laptop.

Turn 7

create a new kaggle.md and dataset to upload and this time it should get better results.

make sure to improve the methods to get better results this time.

delete the previous kaggle.md

Claude’s response & actions

This needs real improvements, not a reshuffled document. Based on the evidence: train loss 0.039 against val CER 18–21% means the model is data-limited, not capacity-limited — so more capacity would just overfit harder. The fix is data, especially for Kannada (3,662 images).

Starting with fonts, since synthetic data is worthless without typeface diversity:

Answering your first question directly, with evidence rather than assertion:

## Separation audit — I found a real defect

I wrote [scripts/leakage_audit.py](scripts/leakage_audit.py) to check four paths independently:

| Check | Result |

|---|---|

| Image leakage (exact bytes) | ✅ 0 of 300 benchmark, 0 of 6,953 test, 0 of 2,601 val |

| Split integrity (shared source photographs) | ✅ 0 across all three pairs |

| Label leakage — benchmark | ❌ 27 of 288 strings appeared as training labels |

| Vocabulary overlap (context) | test 68.5%, benchmark 20.4% of word tokens |

The failure was mine. real_corpus_words() added benchmark words to the index that the coverage phase samples from, so 26 benchmark strings got rendered as synthetic training images — including उपलब्धसंहितानुसारेणैव and त्रैलोक्योद्धृतिदक्षेण, which occur nowhere else. That's telling the model the answer on 10.3% of the scored images.

My docstring claimed "never its words as training text" while the code did exactly that. Worth saying plainly: had you not asked, the v2 benchmark number would have been inflated by an unknown amount and I'd have reported it as clean.

Fixed: the benchmark now contributes its *element inventory* only. Any corpus word matching a benchmark string is excluded from rendering (70 blocked), and the one element known only from the benchmark is covered by a synthetic carrier instead of its own word.

The other 9 shared strings come from the real BSTD training data — ordinary words like काले, तथा, गृह. That's natural language overlap every model has, including Gemini and Qwen, so removing them would be over-correction. It's reported, not scrubbed.

Regeneration is running. When it lands I'll re-run the leakage audit, coverage audit, repackage, and then update KAGGLE.md with the new cells and the audit step in the build chain.

202	## Phase A1 — The label-unit decision ⭐
203	
204	**Partly resolved by measurement — see specs §2.5(b) and plan-A §4.1.** Measured on all 30,947 images:
205	**246 codepoints**, **2,168 grapheme clusters**, ~60 WX symbols.

207	Grapheme clusters are **ruled out**: they cut required CTC timesteps by ~44% but cost a 2,168-class

## [REPORT.md](REPORT.md) — the deliverable

Ten sections, no mention of earlier attempts. Leads with the result table against all eleven systems on the same 300 images:

| System | Params | chrF++ | CER |

|---|---|---|---|

| This model | 2.24 M | 88.6 | 2.65 |

| Gemini 2.5 Flash | undisclosed | 86.3 | 4.4 |

| Claude Opus 4.7 | undisclosed | 82.2 | 5.1 |

| Qwen3-VL-8B (best open) | 8 B | 75.2 | 9.3 |

| EasyOCR | ~30 M | 58.3 | 34.3 |

| DeepSeek-OCR | 3 B | 10.4 | 112.0 |

It covers the benchmark and harness validation, method (architecture, coverage guarantee, leakage audit), the ablation showing the synthetic corpus is worth +16.9, deployment with verified CPU numbers, and a limitations section.

Three things stated rather than buried, because a reader will find them anyway: this is recognition on pre-cropped crops not end-to-end OCR; the synthetic data was designed against benchmark statistics (visible as +16.9 on the benchmark against +2.0 on general Devanagari photographs); and Kannada at 30.3% CER.

I also included the ±1.0 run-to-run variance, which is why S2 reads as *parity* with Gemini rather than a win — a 2.3-point margin on 300 images isn't resolvable.

## [specs.md](specs.md)

  • §1.3b — outcome against all seven criteria, with S5 marked ❌ and explained
  • §2.6–2.9 — fonts resolved, final corpus as built, the Kannada limitation, thin elements
  • Notes that the two-track split in §3.0 proved unnecessary: one model met both the accuracy and latency criteria, so no distillation stage was needed

## [tasks.md](tasks.md)

  • STATUS block at the top — full configuration and results of the trained session
  • Phases 2, A1, A3, A5, 4, 5 marked complete with their measured outcomes
  • Plan B marked superseded, with the reason: it existed to produce a teacher for distillation, and the student turned out better than any available teacher
  • A closing "what is actually left" table — four items, none blocking the write-up

One judgement call worth flagging: I marked S6 (joint vs separate) as partial, not done. It's the one mandatory experiment still open — the deva/kann arms are wired in Cell 11 at 90 epochs each. If your course requires all stated criteria attempted, that's the gap to close.

11/12 exact

The tool is built, tested, and documented. Everything verified working.

## How to use it

# a whole page
python scripts\ocr.py page.jpg

# one pre-cropped word
python scripts\ocr.py word.png --single

# a folder, writing .txt files
python scripts\ocr.py scans\ --out results\

# see what the detector found (use this when output looks wrong)
python scripts\ocr.py page.jpg --boxes marked.png

One-time install: pip install onnxruntime pillow numpy easyocr. After first run it's fully offline.

## The gap you needed filled

Your model reads word crops — it doesn't find words on a page. So [scripts/ocr.py](scripts/ocr.py) pairs it with EasyOCR's CRAFT detector:

page → [detector finds boxes] → each box → [your model reads it] → text

The detector is a separate published model, not part of your work. Worth knowing because page accuracy now depends on both stages.

## Verified

Test pages with known text — every character correct on both scripts:

| | Result |

|---|---|

| Devanagari, 4 lines | all characters correct, 2 spaces lost of ~20 words |

| Kannada, 3 lines | all three lines exact |

Python API, as documented — 11/12 benchmark words exact.

Notable: Kannada is flawless on clean print. Its 30% error rate is a *photography* problem, not a script one — useful to know if your documents are scans rather than phone photos.

## One known quirk

When the detector puts two words in one box, your model reads both correctly but without the space between them — गृह मंत्रालय → गृहमंत्रालय. Characters right, space lost, because training used single-word images. I set the detector to split more aggressively, which fixed most cases.

## Written

[USAGE.md](USAGE.md) — install, commands, expected accuracy, the Python API, troubleshooting, and a "what this does not do" section (no layout analysis, no handwriting, no Latin text, doesn't locate text by itself).

## The three pages

Full A4 pages, ~1,100–1,300 characters each, written for this purpose so the model hasn't seen them:

  • Hindi — administrative notice, 240 words of ordinary prose
  • Sanskrit — classical text with dense conjuncts, word-final viramas and verse marks (the hard one)
  • Kannada — library notice exercising below-baseline conjunct stacking

## Result: 99.78% character accuracy

Across all 3,586 characters, 0.22% character error once word spacing is set aside. Only two genuine recognition errors in the whole set — one dropped virama, one dropped Kannada conjunct.

The raw figure is 1.78%, and the difference is worth understanding: it's the *detector* merging adjacent words into one box, after which your model reads both correctly but without a space. सायं पाँच बजे → सायंपाँचबजे. That's also why word error looks high at 20% — one lost space fails two words.

So: character accuracy is your model's, word segmentation is mostly the detector's. The scorer reports both columns so you can tell them apart.

Per-language accuracy:

| Language | Script | Chars | Character accuracy | Character acc. (ignoring spaces) | Word acc. | Line acc. |

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| Hindi | Devanagari | 1,323 | 97.35% | 99.91% | 73.75% | 34.6% |

| Sanskrit | Devanagari | 1,105 | 98.46% | 99.79% | 78.63% | 60.9% |

| Kannada | Kannada | 1,158 | 98.96% | 99.61% | 86.92% | 64.0% |

| All three | both | 3,586 | 98.22% | 99.78% | 79.51% | 52.2% |

By script: Devanagari 99.86%, Kannada 99.61% (ignoring spaces).

## Reading these

The second column is the model's own accuracy — 99.6–99.9% for all three languages. The first column is lower only because of lost spaces, which come from the detector merging adjacent words, not from misreading characters.

Hindi looks worst in column one and best in column two. That isn't a contradiction: its page has the longest lines and most words, so more chances for the detector to merge two of them. Its actual character reading is the most accurate of the three at 99.91%.

Word accuracy is much lower than character accuracy everywhere, because a word counts only if the spacing around it is also right. One merged pair fails two words. If you need word-level output, that gap is the detector's to close, not the model's.

Line accuracy is the harshest measure — a whole line must be perfect. Hindi's 34.6% reflects its longer lines, where one merge anywhere fails the line.

## One caveat worth keeping in view

These are clean rendered pages. On photographs of real documents the measured figures are much lower — 82.2% character accuracy for Devanagari and 70.0% for Kannada on signboard photography. Scans and born-digital PDFs behave like the table above; phone photos do not.

The scorer now prints all of this, and [testing/README.md](testing/README.md) §4 documents it.

# Bharat Scene Text Dataset: Key Extraction

## (1) Per-Language WRR and CRR for Kannada and Hindi

**Kannada:**
- WRR: 0.29
- CRR: 0.48

**Hindi (merged with Marathi as "Devanagari"):**
- WRR: 0.39
- CRR: 0.54

## (2) Recognition Models Benchmarked

The primary model evaluated was PARSeq, with results showing "0.79" for Assamese, "0.82" for Bengali, and "0.92" for English when trained on BSTD. Comparative baselines included CRNN from AI4Bharat and off-the-shelf tools (Tesseract, PaddleOCR, EasyOCR), though "PARSeq is used in our proposed baseline."

## (3) Hardest Languages and Reasons

The paper identifies that "performance drops noticeably for other languages: Marathi (86%), Bengali (82%), Tamil (80%)." For lower-resource languages, Telugu and Malayalam showed particular challenges, with "recognition accuracies remain in the 50% range." The text notes difficulty stems from script complexity and limited training data availability.

# SVTRv2X: Architecture and Performance Details

## 1. Self-Distilled Mixture-of-Experts Architecture

**Architecture Overview:**
The MoE module replaces standard MLPs within Mixing blocks. It consists of "a lightweight gating network and N parallel expert sub-networks" that enable sparse token routing.

**Expert Routing Mechanism:**
The gating network computes scores for each expert via a linear transformation. "Only the top-K experts (typically K = 1 or 2) are selected for each token." In their experiments, they use 4 experts with top-1 routing strategy. The routed output combines outputs from selected experts, with each expert typically implemented as "a two-layer feed-forward module."

The self-distillation component transfers knowledge from deeper to shallower layers using feature projections and convolutions, with deeper features supervising shallower ones during a two-phase training strategy.

## 2. Accuracy Gains Over SVTRv2

**Specific improvements on Chinese Text Recognition:**
- Average across all datasets: **+2.19%** (79.45% → 81.64% for SVTRv2X-T)
- Scene dataset: **+3.3%** (77.8% → 81.1%)
- Web dataset: **+1.2%** (80.0% → 81.2%)
- Handwriting dataset: **+2.9%** (62.0%
… [878 more characters]

This is directly on your question about combining two tuned models:

▸ WebFetch: Extract the PSMC (Pre-train, Specialize, Merge, Co-train) method: (1) exactly how language-specific experts are created and merged via task arithmetic, (2) whic…
# PSMC Method Extraction

## (1) Expert Creation and Merging via Task Arithmetic

**Creation:** Language-specific experts derive from a pre-trained English anchor model (θ_En) through fine-tuning on language-specific data. Each target language ℓ receives its own specialist model θ_ℓ.

**Merging Formula:** Task vectors capture the directional shift: τ_ℓ = θ_ℓ - θ_En. The merged model combines these vectors:

> "θ_Merged = θ_En + (1/L)∑τ_ℓ"

This averages the specialized weight offsets across all L language experts while maintaining the common English foundation.

## (2) Layer Specialization: Evidence and Split

**Shared Lower Layers:** Early encoder layers (lower indices) demonstrate "very similar" representations across all specialist models, shown via Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) analysis in Figure 1.

**Script-Specific Upper Layers:** Higher layers "diverge to capture unique script nuances" and "language-specific features are learned in the deeper layers."

This hierarchical structure justifies the merging strategy: "lower layers learn redundant, highly similar features" while upper layers remain specialized.

## (3) Quantitative Accuracy Gains

**Mozhi Dataset Results (Table

Let me state the central finding now, because it reframes the whole question.

## "90%" isn't one number — it's three very different targets

Your model is measured on three kinds of data, and they are not comparable:

| Data | Our current accuracy | Published state of the art |

|---|---|---|

| Clean printed PDFs (your test pages) | 99.6–99.9% characters | — |

| Printed books/newspapers (Mozhi corpus) | untested | 96–97% word accuracy |

| Scene-text photographs (BSTD signboards) | 70% Kannada, 82% Devanagari chars | 48% Kannada, 54% Devanagari |

That last row is the important one. I checked the Bharat Scene Text paper's own published baseline — PARSeq, a far larger and stronger model, on the same corpus your Kannada test set comes from:

| | Your model | BSTD paper's PARSeq |

|---|---|---|

| Kannada CRR / WRR | 0.700 / 0.328 | 0.48 / 0.29 |

| Devanagari CRR / WRR | 0.823 / 0.614 | 0.54 / 0.39 |

You are already 22 points above published state of the art on Kannada scene text. Getting that to 90% isn't a tuning problem — no published system is within 40 points of it. Signboard photographs of Kannada are simply not a solved problem.

But on printed documents — which is what your project is actually for — 90% is not only achievable, it's below what's been published. The 2026 CVIT work reports 97.4% word accuracy across 13 Indic languages on printed text.

So the honest reframing: you're chasing 90% on the wrong dataset. Let me finish the measurements and lay out what actually moves the needle.

[KAGGLE_v3.md](KAGGLE_v3.md) is written and the pipeline is building. Summary of what you asked:

## Yes — single model, unchanged architecture

Mozhi adds training data only. Same 2.24 M CRNN+CTC, same 302 classes, one .onnx reading all three languages. v2 weights untouched — v3 writes to separate files so you can compare and fall back.

## One correction on Sanskrit

Mozhi has 12 languages but no Sanskrit. You get:

| | Mozhi provides |

|---|---|

| Kannada | ~100,000 real printed images (vs your 3,662) |

| Hindi | ~100,000 |

| Marathi | ~100,000 — same Devanagari glyphs |

| Sanskrit | nothing directly |

Sanskrit still benefits from the shared Devanagari glyph inventory, but its distinguishing feature — conjunct density 4× Hindi's — isn't in this corpus. Your existing Sanskrit data and the synthetic trailing-mark injection remain its specific ingredients, and both stay in the mix.

## You won't re-upload the 543 MB

The trainer now searches multiple mounted directories. So Mozhi ships as a second Kaggle dataset you attach alongside the existing one — --extra <mozhi dir> --mozhi. Verified backward-compatible: the v2 command still runs identically.

## What the run gives you that v2 couldn't

A printed-domain test set. Every number so far is either clean synthetic renders or signboard photos. Mozhi's test split is real printed document text — the actual use case — and the published bar there is ~97% word accuracy, which is a bar that exists, unlike 90% on signboards.

## Two things to watch

Epochs: 25, not 45. v3 has ~410,000 images → ~6,400 steps/epoch, so 25 epochs is ~160,000 steps — more optimisation than v2 got on three times the data.

Sanskrit regression. Adding 300,000 printed Hindi and Kannada images shifts the distribution away from Sanskrit. If the benchmark drops more than ~2 chrF++ (your measured noise floor), that's real and you'd weight the Sanskrit corpus up or keep v2 for Sanskrit.

▸ Bash: Read existing shared element module
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
One definition of a "script element", shared by the generator and the auditor.

Kept in one file on purpose: if the generator and the auditor disagree about
what an element is, the audit silently stops meaning anything. That already
happened once here - the auditor counted `य् + ।` (a word-final virama followed
by a danda) as a conjunct, reported it as an uncovered element, and there was
no way to cover it because it is punctuation, not a consonant cluster.

An element is one of three things the model must learn to see:

  codepoint    the base inventory
  vowel-sign   consonant + matra; the sign wraps around the consonant and
               changes its whole visual envelope
  conjunct     consonant + virama + consonant, drawn as one fused or stacked
               glyph that often resembles neither input. Kannada's vattakshara
               sits *below* the base consonant.

Only real consonants may take part in a conjunct. Punctuation, digits, vowels
and matras are excluded, because no font ligates them and no document contains
them fused.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import unicodedata

RANGES = {"devanagari": range(0x0900, 0x0980), "kannad
… [686 more characters]

Downloads are chugging along. Let me look at the model definition so the v4 trainer changes are concrete rather than hand-wavy:

▸ Bash: Read the CRNN architecture
class CRNN(nn.Module):
    """CNN over the image, sequence model over the width axis, CTC head.

    Total width downsampling is exactly 4 (two 2x2 pools), matching STRIDE.
    Height is reduced to 1 by pooling and a final height-collapsing conv.
    """

    def __init__(self, n_classes: int, neck: str = "lstm", width: int = 256):
        super().__init__()
        self.cnn = nn.Sequential(
            conv_bn(1, 32), conv_bn(32, 32),
            nn.MaxPool2d(2, 2),                       # 24 x W/2
            conv_bn(32, 64), conv_bn(64, 64),
            nn.MaxPool2d(2, 2),                       # 12 x W/4
            conv_bn(64, 128), conv_bn(128, 128),
            nn.MaxPool2d((2, 1), (2, 1)),             # 6 x W/4
            conv_bn(128, width), conv_bn(width, width),
            nn.MaxPool2d((2, 1), (2, 1)),             # 3 x W/4
            nn.Conv2d(width, width, (3, 1), 1, 0, bias=False),  # 1 x W/4
            nn.BatchNorm2d(width), nn.ReLU(inplace=True),
        )
        self.neck_kind = neck
        if neck == "lstm":
            self.neck = nn.LSTM(width, width // 2, num_layers=2,
                                bidirectional=True, batch_first=True, dropout=0.1)
    
… [809 more characters]

# Research report: Why LLMs/VLMs fail on Indic scripts, and what interpretability you can actually run

Scope note on evidence quality. I have flagged every claim with one of:
**[S]** solid — peer-reviewed or multiply-corroborated; **[M]** moderate — arXiv preprint with concrete measured numbers but no independent replication; **[W]** weak — single unreplicated preprint, blog, or my inference. A large fraction of the most on-point tokenisation work is 2026 arXiv preprints by single authors; treat those as "cite for the number, do not lean the argument on them."

---

# PART A — Mechanistic explanations for Indic failure

## A1. Tokenisation

### A1.1 The mechanism worth building the paper around: pre-tokenisation, not BPE

The strongest and most *mechanistic* result here is not "Indic is rare in the corpus". It is a **regex bug inherited from GPT-2**.

GPT-2's ByteLevel pre-tokeniser splits on `\p{L}+` (Unicode category *Letter* only). In abugidas, dependent vowel signs (matras), anusvara, nukta, virama are Unicode categories **Mn** (non-spacing mark) and **Mc** (spacing combining mark) — *not* L. So `\p{L}+` splits every Devanagari/Tamil/Kannada word at **every single vowel sign**
… [12,848 more characters]

# PART B — Interpretability techniques you can actually run

## B0. Master table

| Grad-CAM family | Which CNN feature-map regions drive a chosen emission | gradients | Yes, with a scalar target | No | ms/sample | `pytorch-grad-cam`, `torchcam` |

▸ Bash: Read error taxonomy and venue guidance
## B6. Error-taxonomy methodology

**Recommended scheme (there is no single canonical published standard — say so):**

Layer 1 — **alignment level.** Report CER/WER at three granularities and report the deltas:
 (a) NFC codepoint, (b) **UAX #29 extended grapheme cluster**, (c) **akshara / orthographic syllable** via the LREC-COLING 2024 grapheme parser (Ansary et al., https://aclanthology.org/2024.lrec-main.1479/). The (a)→(b) gap quantifies how much of your "error" is combining-mark bookkeeping.

Layer 2 — **edit type.** Substitution / insertion / deletion (+ transposition if you align at grapheme level). Precedent that error *distributions* are script-specific: Kurdish Sorani script-aware OCR reports S 41% / D 27% / I 32% (https://www.mdpi.com/1999-4893/19/9/795 [M]) — i.e. the Latin-centric assumption that substitutions dominate does not hold.

Layer 3 — **script-aware linguistic category** (the abugida-specific layer). Use the six categories from the Devanagari benchmark: **conjunct, nukta, anusvara, matra placement, numeral, look-alike**. Extend with: **virama/halant handling**, **ZWJ/ZWNJ**, **pre-base reordering**, **script switch / transliteration**.

## What's built

Data — 12 languages, 9 scripts, verified

| Corpus | Status |

|---|---|

| Mozhi printed, 12 languages | done — 4.5 GB, 36 archives, all valid, 1.2M word images |

| BSTD scene, all 12 languages | done — 791 MB, 106,490 entries (we previously had images for only 3 languages) |

| IIIT-IndicSTR-Word, 250k scene crops | downloading, 1 of 10 |

Code

  • [scripts/indic_scripts.py](scripts/indic_scripts.py) — one definition of all nine scripts, imported by every builder. It verifies itself against Unicode: python scripts/indic_scripts.py checks each derived consonant set and virama against its Unicode name. This exists because a rule living in one builder and not another has already bitten this project twice.
  • [kaggle/train_ocr_v4.py](kaggle/train_ocr_v4.py) — v3's trainer is untouched. v4 adds pack_padded_sequence (fixes the padding bug we measured), collapses 6 feature rows instead of 3 before the sequence head, and takes --roadside, --weight-decay, --height, --scripts all.
  • [scripts/download_roadside.py](scripts/download_roadside.py) — reproducible, resumable.

Two bugs caught during the build, both of which would have failed on Kaggle: sys used but never imported in the v4 trainer, and the height-collapse kernel being hardcoded to 48px input so --height 64 would have produced a shape error rather than a result.

## On the language count

You said 10; the plan is 12 languages across 9 scripts. Those aren't in conflict — the roadside scene corpus covers 10 languages, Mozhi covers 12, and OCR works per-script, so Hindi+Marathi are one script and Bengali+Assamese+Manipuri are another. Urdu is excluded, and not as a preference: Mozhi ships it as *line* images with no word annotations, so it doesn't fit a word-based loader at all.

## The explainability research — the finding worth building the paper on

Not "Indic is rare in training data." It's a regex inherited from GPT-2.

GPT-2's pre-tokeniser splits on \p{L}+ — Unicode category *Letter*. But matras, anusvara, nukta and virama are categories Mn/Mc, not L. So it splits every Devanagari, Tamil and Kannada word at every vowel sign, before BPE training even starts. BPE merges never cross a pre-token boundary, so this is a *training-free lower bound* on token inflation that no extra corpus can fix.

Kannada: 6.09 tokens/word under the broken regex, 1.25 under a mark-aware one — 4.87×. Broken: GPT-3.5/4, Llama-3, Qwen2.5. Fixed: GPT-4o, Gemma-2.

The strongest *novel* experiment is a CTC-lens: run the trained CTC head on each intermediate layer. If consonants are readable from the CNN alone but matras only after the recurrence, that's a mechanistic reason why patch-based encoders fail on exactly those categories — and it turns the v4 architecture change into a prediction rather than a tweak.

Full plan in [reports/PAPER_PLAN.md](reports/PAPER_PLAN.md), including the counter-evidence that must go in (fertility does *not* reliably predict downstream performance — two solid papers say so, and omitting that would get the paper rejected).

## Two problems found and handled

The builder would have run out of memory. Your machine had 3.1 GB free and the v3 builder accumulated every image in RAM before writing — about 5 GB at twelve languages. I rewrote it to stream through ParquetWriter. Measured peak: 0.46 GB. It would have crashed partway and left corrupt shards.

Two roadside archives were silently truncated — odia at 4 of 140 MB and telugu at 79 of 168 MB. The downloader reported "10 archives" because the files existed; only a size check against the server caught it. The roadside build crashed on the bad gzip, which is how I found it. Both are resuming now, and I cleared the partial shards it left behind since the writers never closed cleanly.

▸ Bash: Review the modified forward pass
    def forward(self, x: torch.Tensor, lengths: torch.Tensor | None = None,
                aux: bool = False):
        f = self.cnn(x)                 # B, C, 1, T
        f = f.squeeze(2).permute(0, 2, 1)   # B, T, C
        conv = f                        # pre-recurrence, for the aux head
        if self.neck_kind == "lstm":
            if lengths is None:
                f, _ = self.neck(f)
            else:
                # A bidirectional LSTM's backward pass starts at the last
                # timestep. On a padded batch that is padding, and the
                # contaminated state carries into the real text. Packing makes
                # the backward direction start at each item's true final step.
                # lengths is omitted at export time, so ONNX is unaffected.
                packed = nn.utils.rnn.pack_padded_sequence(
                    f, lengths.clamp(min=1).cpu(), batch_first=True,
                    enforce_sorted=False)
                out, _ = self.neck(packed)
                f, _ = nn.utils.rnn.pad_packed_sequence(
                    out, batch_first=True, total_length=f.size(1))
        else:
            f = self.neck(f)
        out = self.head
… [183 more characters]

▸ Bash: Rename colliding local and check return
renamed inner 'out' -> 'packed_out' (collided with the return value)

        out = self.head(f)              # B, T, n_classes
        if self.training and aux:
            # (main, auxiliary) - the caller adds the second CTC term.
            return out, self.aux_head(conv)
        return out

## One accuracy change added

An auxiliary CTC head on the convolutional features, active during training only. It forces the CNN to make characters linearly separable itself rather than leaving all discrimination to the LSTM, and gives the conv stack a short gradient path.

Measured elsewhere at −0.3 to −0.8pp CER (Retsinas et al.), +1.82pp (PP-OCRv3 GTC), +2.28pp (SVTRv2 SGM). It's dropped at inference — forward() only returns it while training, so the ONNX export never traces it.

Model is 2.86M during training, 2.61M deployed. Worth stating precisely in the report, since size is part of the claim.

--no-aux-ctc ablates it, and noaux is in the tuning menu so you can measure what it actually bought rather than trusting the papers.

## Run it

Cell 1 → Cell 3 → Cell 4 → Cell 5.

Don't skip Cell 4 this time — it now trains *through* the auxiliary head, which is the only way that new gradient path gets tested before six hours are committed. Expect ~2.86M parameters at Cell 3, and 16/16 by step 750 at Cell 4.

The research is decisive on one point: nobody has beaten the Mozhi CRNN baselines on printed Indic word recognition in 2026, and those are the numbers to measure against. ## Bugs fixed in the md

My first audit missed the second one because it searched for the literal len(cs)+1 and this said len(c2)+1. I rewrote it to match the pattern and to validate every checkpoint key against the real schema. It reports clean now.

## One accuracy change

An auxiliary CTC head on the conv features, training only, dropped at inference. Measured at +1.8 to +2.3pp elsewhere. Model is 2.86M training / 2.61M deployed. --no-aux-ctc ablates it.

## Where we actually stand

Nobody has beaten the Mozhi CRNN baselines on printed Indic word recognition in 2026. Those are still the numbers to beat, and they're now in §6a of the doc so you can read Cell 7 against them directly. The hardest target is Kannada at 88.7% word accuracy — the weakest script in their table, and the one v3 already handled well.

Scene text is the opposite story: BSTD-finetuned PARSeq averages 0.73 word accuracy and v3 managed 0.336 on Kannada. That's the gap the 250k roadside crops are meant to close.

But their test split leaks, and I verified it. Mozhi's paper claims book-disjoint test in §6.1 and random 80:10:10 in §3. Reading the released ground truth settles it — train and test share every book in all twelve languages, and 723–1,573 individual scanned pages each. Their published numbers measure reading further words off pages already trained on.

So report both: printed_seen against their Table 3 (like-for-like, same protocol), and printed_unseen from withdrawn books as the honest number. Expect the second to be lower. Claiming only the higher one would repeat their mistake.

## The claim worth making

Not "beat SOTA everywhere". This:

> No published single model covers 8+ Indic scripts for printed word recognition. Mozhi's baselines are 13 separate models. Every multi-script Indic system published in 2026 is ≥85M parameters, or ≥0.8B for the production ones. A 2.61M unified model across nine scripts is 33× smaller than the smallest published multi-script Indic OCR model.

Two honesty constraints, both in the doc: Mozhi's parameter count is unpublished (~8.3M is my computation from their architecture), and UniLipi's numbers are handwritten manuscripts, so we can't claim a win over them.

## Run it

Cell 1 → 3 → 4 → 5. Don't skip Cell 4 — it now trains through the auxiliary head, the only new gradient path. Expect ~2.86M at Cell 3 and 16/16 by step 750.

▸ Bash: Inspect the offline CLI and check web deps
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Offline Devanagari / Kannada OCR — command-line tool.

    python scripts/ocr.py page.jpg                 # a whole page -> text
    python scripts/ocr.py word.png --single        # one pre-cropped word
    python scripts/ocr.py scans/ --out results/    # a folder, batch
    python scripts/ocr.py page.jpg --boxes out.png # also draw what it found

Runs entirely on the CPU with no network connection.

How it works, and where the honest limit is
-------------------------------------------
This project trained a **recogniser**: it reads a word image and returns text.
It does not locate words on a page. So for full pages this tool pairs it with
EasyOCR's text *detector* (CRAFT), which is a separate published model, and uses
our recogniser for the reading:

    page -> [EasyOCR CRAFT detector] -> word boxes -> [our model] -> text

The accuracy figures in REPORT.md measure only the second stage, on word images
that were already cut out. End-to-end page accuracy will be lower, because a
word the detector misses is a word no recogniser can read. With `--single` the
detector is skipped entirely and the reported figures apply directly.

Requirements (all already in
… [787 more characters]

The web app is built, tested, and running right now at http://127.0.0.1:8000 — open it.

## What it does

Drop in a PNG, JPG or PDF, press Read page. You get the recognised text, the page with word boxes tinted by the model's confidence, and a copy button. Nothing leaves your machine.

Measured on your three test PDFs:

| | words | time | mean confidence |

|---|---|---|---|

| hindi.pdf | 238 | 1.45 s | 0.988 |

| kannada.pdf | 131 | 1.08 s | 0.978 |

| sanskrit.pdf | 128 | 0.39 s | 0.985 |

## Two design decisions worth knowing

No PyTorch. scripts/ocr.py uses EasyOCR's CRAFT detector, which works better on hard pages but installs ~2 GB of PyTorch to draw boxes. For a demo of a 10 MB model that's the wrong trade, so segment.py finds words with Otsu thresholding and projection profiles in pure numpy — 38 ms per page. Total dependencies under 100 MB.

Both sliders were tuned by measurement, not taste. *Word spacing* defaults to 0.15 because sweeping it showed Hindi and Sanskrit stable up to 0.15 and Kannada swinging from 492 to 133 words — Devanagari joins characters under the shirorekha while Kannada doesn't. I also tried a per-line adaptive threshold and it was *worse*, so the fixed ratio stayed.

*Min confidence* exists because of a real bug I hit: the Hindi page produced a stray Tamil letter ல from a 24×14 pixel speck. Geometric filters couldn't catch it — too small to look like a rule, too ordinary to look like noise — but the model scored it 0.216 against 0.9+ for every real word. Filtering on the model's own confidence removed it cleanly.

## Where it fails, and the README says so

Scene photographs (0.45 vs a specialist's 0.73), skewed scans, multi-column layouts, handwriting. A demo that hides its limits is worse than one that doesn't, so those are in [app/README.md](app/README.md) and in the UI itself.

## Files

app/server.py      FastAPI, loads the ONNX once
app/segment.py     page -> word boxes, numpy + Pillow only
app/static/index.html   whole UI, no build step, no framework
app/README.md      how to run it, why the defaults are what they are

To restart later: python app\server.py. One note — the server holds port 8000, and pkill doesn't stop it on Windows; use Ctrl-C in its terminal, or Get-Process python | Stop-Process -Force.

## One real bug caught while testing

I first hardcoded gemini-2.5-flash, and the API replied "no longer available to new users". Pinned flash versions go stale on Google's schedule. The default is now gemini-flash-latest, an alias that always resolves to the current model — so this won't break again in three months.

## How to make a key, for when you need another

1. https://aistudio.google.com/apikey

2. Sign in → Create API key → pick a project

3. Copy it (starts AIza...)

Free tier, no card. One page is one request.

To use it:

setx GEMINI_API_KEY "AIza..."     # permanent; open a NEW terminal after

Or put it in app\.gemini_key as a single line.

The key is never in any source file — only env var or that file, which you never share. If it leaks, delete it in AI Studio and make a new one.

## Two things worth knowing for the demo

Translation is a separate, much larger model. Your OCR model is 2.61M parameters and reads glyphs — it has no idea what words mean. Keeping them separate is deliberate; claiming one model did both would misrepresent what you built. The UI says so too.

It's the only time the app touches the network, and it sends *text only*, never the image. Worth saying out loud in a demo about document OCR.

Timing: OCR 1.1 s locally, Gemini round trip 13.9 s. Translation is the slow half, and that's your connection, not your laptop.

Everything is in [app/README.md](app/README.md) — including what each failure mode means, since each one reports its actual cause rather than a generic error.

Turn 23

please check this tamil conversion accuracy too.

Claude’s response & actions

This one is much worse, and the cause is different — it's not the recogniser.

## Roughly 25–30% of words correct

But look at *what* went wrong:

| page | recognised |

|---|---|

| மன்னும் | மன்னு · ம் |

| இமய | இ · ம · ய |

| மலையெங்கள் | மலை · வெ · ய · ங்க · ள் |

| போற்பிறி | பே · ◌ாற் · பிறி |

Those aren't misreadings. They're one word cut into pieces, and each piece read correctly. இ, ம, ய are the right characters — just boxed separately. And standalone ◌ா marks in the output are the giveaway: a crop that contained only a vowel sign.

When a word is bounded correctly, it's read correctly — மலையே, நூலெங்கள், நூலே, எமக்கிலை, ஈடே, வீரர், மலிந்தநன், நல்லன, நாடுறு, பூரண, நன்னாடு all came out exact. The 2.61M model is doing its job; the segmenter in front of it is not.

## Why Tamil specifically breaks it

Two reasons compound:

1. Tamil's vowel signs wrap around the consonant. போ is one codepoint (U+0BCB) but renders as three visual pieces: ெ to the *left* of ப, and ா to the *right*. A vertical cut between them splits a single character into பெ and ா — which is exactly பே ◌ாற் in your output. No gap-based segmenter can get this right without knowing the script.

2. Tamil doesn't connect its characters. Devanagari joins them under the shirorekha, so almost any gap is a word break. Tamil leaves real gaps between characters, and in this large, wide-spaced face the inter-character gaps are about as wide as the inter-word ones. There's no threshold that separates them.

## What to do right now

Raise the "word spacing" slider — try 0.30, then 0.40. That merges aggressively and will recover most of these words. It's the exact control for this failure, and the Malayalam and Tamil pages are why I made it adjustable rather than fixed.

## The honest framing for your report

This is the cost of the design choice I made: a projection-profile segmenter in pure numpy instead of EasyOCR's CRAFT detector, to avoid a 2 GB PyTorch dependency for a 10 MB model. It works well on dense printed prose and fails on large decorative type in scripts with disconnected glyphs.

Worth separating clearly in the writeup: your recogniser scores 98.6% character accuracy on held-out Tamil books. This page scores ~30% at the *word* level because of detection, which is a different component and not what you trained. Most OCR papers report only the recogniser number on pre-cropped words for exactly this reason — and scripts/ocr.py --single is the honest path when you want to measure just the model.

If you send me this image file I'll sweep the slider on it and tell you which value recovers the most, and I can also make the default ratio script-aware — detect the script from a first pass, then re-segment with a value suited to it.

Turn 24

I want to know how well this will work on hand-written images or pdfs..

or should I use it only for typed pdf's and images..

Claude’s response & actions

Use it for typed documents. Handwriting is an interesting finding for your report, not a capability you should rely on.

## What the evidence actually says

| Input | character accuracy | usable? |

|---|---|---|

| Printed book scans (training domain) | 98–99.8% | yes |

| Printed, unfamiliar decorative typeface | 91.4% | yes, with the spacing slider |

| Neat handwriting, well-separated letters | 89.9% | no — see below |

| Faster, connected handwriting | 38.5% | no |

## Why 89.9% on neat handwriting still isn't usable

The accuracy number is seductive and misleading. The real problem on your handwritten page was that 7 of 21 lines were never transcribed at all — 33% of the page silently missing.

That's a worse failure than being wrong. A wrong word is visible; a missing line looks like the page just ended. For a document tool, silent omission is the one failure you can't design around.

And the 89.9% only held for the top third. The same page, same pen, same writer, dropped to 38.5% where the writing sped up — so you can't predict in advance whether a given page will work.

## The actual boundary

It isn't "printed versus handwritten". It's how separated the characters are:

  • Works: dark text on light background, straight horizontal lines, visible gaps between words, characters not touching
  • Fails: connected or cursive strokes, varying baselines, letters running together

That's why neat handwriting scored 90% and fast handwriting scored 38%. Your model reads *glyphs*; the segmenter in front of it needs *gaps*. Connected writing destroys the gaps, and once a line can't be split into words, nothing downstream can recover.

## Practical rule for the demo

Typed PDFs and scans — use it, quote the numbers, it's genuinely strong.

Handwriting — show it as an experiment, not a feature. The honest framing is actually more interesting: *trained exclusively on print, it transfers to neat handwriting at ~90% character accuracy and collapses on connected writing* — which demonstrates where the domain boundary sits. That's a better talking point than a capability claim you'd have to walk back when someone hands you a messy page.

## One thing that helps you judge any page

The box colours in the app are tinted by the model's own confidence — green above 0.9, amber above 0.7, red below. On a page it handles well, boxes are nearly all green. On the handwritten page you'd see amber and red spreading as you go down. If a page comes back mostly green, trust it; mostly amber, don't.

What the app can't show you is the lines it never boxed at all. Worth eyeballing the box overlay against the original before trusting any unusual page.

## If you did want handwriting

The data exists — IIIT-INDIC-HW-WORDS: 872,000 handwritten words, 10 scripts including Tamil, 135 writers. Adding it would be a v5 training run, roughly the same effort as v4. Not something to attempt before your demo.

Front-end rebuilt, and removing the sliders turned out to fix a real problem rather than just tidy up. Server is running at http://127.0.0.1:8000.

## The Tamil page went from broken to near-perfect

| | words out | CER | words exact |

|---|---|---|---|

| before — fixed slider at 0.15 | 127 (for 66 words) | 18.0% | 27/66 |

| after — automatic | 69 | 1.1% | 61/66 |

98.9% character accuracy, up from 82%. Same model, same image. The only change is that the app now picks the word-spacing value itself.

## The three sliders, and what happened to each

Word spacing — kept, but now automatic. This was the one that mattered: it decides where one word ends and the next begins. A fixed value can't work, because a wide-tracked Tamil face needs roughly twice what a Devanagari book page does. The app now segments the page at seven different values and picks where the word count *stops changing* — that plateau is exactly where the threshold sits between gaps *inside* a word and gaps *between* words. It costs ~280 ms and needs no knowledge of the script, which matters because Kannada wants the same value as Devanagari while Tamil doesn't. A manual override lives under Advanced.

Minimum confidence — removed entirely. This one was actively harmful. It was deleting genuine Tamil vowel signs: the segmenter orphaned a ா, the model was unsure of a lone mark, and the filter silently dropped the character. Replaced with a rule that requires a crop to be both low-confidence and unusually small — a speck is tiny *and* unsure; a short real word is tiny but confident; a stray vowel sign is unsure but not unusually small. The Hindi page's 24×14 speck is still caught (1 dropped), and no vowel signs are lost.

PDF render dpi — moved to Advanced. 200 dpi works for everything tested. It only matters for scans of very small print, so it shouldn't occupy the main interface.

So: zero sliders in the main view. Three buttons, one checkbox.

## Other front-end changes

Proper typography (Noto Sans/Serif, which actually render Indic well), light and dark themes, a confidence legend explaining what the box colours mean, mean confidence shown per page, and the chosen word-spacing value surfaced in the stats bar so you can see what it decided.

One addition to the explanatory note worth mentioning in your demo: text with no box around it was never read at all, which is easier to miss than a wrong word — that was the real failure on the handwritten page, where a third of the lines simply vanished.

▸ Bash: Check the renderer and training fonts
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Correct Devanagari / Kannada text rendering via HarfBuzz + FreeType.

Why this file exists
--------------------
Pillow only shapes complex scripts when it is built against libraqm, and the
Windows wheels (and most Linux wheels) are not. Without shaping, PIL draws
glyphs in codepoint order, which for an abugida is simply wrong:

  * the Devanagari short-i matra (ि) is stored AFTER its consonant but must be
    drawn BEFORE it - unshaped output puts it on the wrong side;
  * conjuncts never ligate, so क् + त renders as क, a visible virama, and त
    instead of the single stacked glyph क्त;
  * Kannada vattaksharas never move below the base consonant.

The resulting images look like plausible Indic text to a reader who does not
know the script, so the fault passes review and silently teaches the model
character sequences that appear in no real document.

This module shapes with HarfBuzz (the same engine used by browsers, Android
and LibreOffice) and rasterises the positioned glyphs with FreeType, so the
synthetic data matches what a real printed page looks like.

    from indic_render import IndicRenderer
    r = IndicRenderer("data/raw/fonts/NotoSansDevanag
… [405 more characters]
▸ Bash: Find Indic fonts not used in training
system Indic fonts available, and whether they were used in training:

Indic-capable Windows fonts NOT used in training:

  devanagari   8  ['Baloo-Regular.ttf', 'Kalam-Bold.ttf', 'Kalam-Light.ttf', 'Nirmala.ttc']

  bengali      1  ['Nirmala.ttc']

▸ Bash: Compare scanned crops against my renders, same words
script          n    scanned crop     my render   verdict

That settles it, and it's a genuinely important finding. The renderer is proven correct — Devanagari and Kannada read my renders at 0.43–1.48% CER, which couldn't happen if the glyphs were malformed. So the Tamil/Telugu/Bengali failures are the model, not my test.

▸ Write: E:\AIDS\Project\indic-document-ocr\reports\BLIND_TEST.md
File created successfully at: E:\AIDS\Project\indic-document-ocr\reports\BLIND_TEST.md (file state is current in your context — no need to Read it back)

I built an independent test set and it found something the earlier evaluations couldn't. Full write-up in [reports/BLIND_TEST.md](reports/BLIND_TEST.md).

## How I kept it clean

Text the model never trained on, in 16 fonts that appear nowhere in the 89 used for training, generated after the model was frozen. I hashed all 1,338,687 training images and checked every test image against them:

test images identical to a training image     0 of 5,381
fonts shared with training                    0 of 16
pseudo-words that spell real training words  19  -> all removed

Two sets. Pseudo-words — valid orthography built from each script's own consonants and vowel signs, but not words in any language, so they can't be guessed from context and leakage is impossible by construction. Real words from held-out books in unseen typefaces.

## The headline

| | pseudo CER | real CER | real char acc |

|---|---|---|---|

| Kannada | 5.12 | 0.53 | 99.5% |

| Devanagari | 8.45 | 1.75 | 98.3% |

| Gujarati | 24.84 | 6.02 | 94.0% |

| Odia / Gurmukhi | ~34 | 12–14 | 86–88% |

| Malayalam / Bengali | ~32 | 14–21 | 79–86% |

| Tamil / Telugu | ~38 | 23–24 | 76–77% |

Well below the 97–99.8% on held-out Mozhi books. That gap is the finding.

## Why — and I verified it rather than guessed

The obvious worry is that my renderer is broken and I'm scoring my own bug. So I read the same words twice — once from the real scanned crop, once from a fresh render:

| | scanned | rendered |

|---|---|---|

| Kannada | 0.87% | 0.43% |

| Devanagari | 0.57% | 1.48% |

| Tamil | 6.87% | 25.44% |

| Telugu | 2.53% | 23.62% |

| Bengali | 2.37% | 21.03% |

Devanagari and Kannada read my renders *as well as real scans*. The renderer is fine — if it produced malformed glyphs, those two would fail too.

The split is exactly the synthetic training corpus. Devanagari and Kannada are the only scripts with synth_* shards. The other seven saw nothing but scanned book crops and photographs, so clean digital type is a domain they've never met.

This is the limitation I recorded in meta.json during the build — now measured. It costs more than I assumed: not just rare-conjunct coverage, but the ability to read clean rendered type at all.

## One more thing worth knowing

Across all scripts, CER is 27.6% on strings that can't be guessed versus 13.0% on real words. The model gets roughly 15 points of accuracy from context — the BiLSTM acting as an implicit language model. Normal for sequence models, but it means proper nouns, numbers and loanwords read materially worse than the headline suggests.

## What this changes

For the demo and report, the honest claim is: 97–99.8% on printed book scans across nine scripts, beating the published baselines on 9 of 9 — and on clean rendered type, only the two scripts with synthetic training hold up.

The fix is clear and the tooling already exists: generate synthetic data for the remaining seven scripts, same pipeline. This blind set is now the thing that would prove whether it worked.

Written for: your course demo audience — a professor and classmates who know ML but not Indic OCR.

The deck: https://claude.ai/artifact/Uuem2A2RqyJFz4FW5ikpLP — 24 slides, with speaker notes on every one. It's private; share it from the page's Share menu if anyone else needs to open it.

## Results, consolidated and verified

Everything now lives in [reports/RESULTS.json](reports/RESULTS.json), rebuilt by [scripts/collect_results.py](scripts/collect_results.py) — nothing retyped from scrollback. Re-measured just now:

model   2,605,741 params deployed · 10.43 MB · 973 classes
speed   5.86 ms/word · 171 words/s on CPU
train   15 epochs · 4.15 h · selected epoch 15
vs Mozhi CRNN   8/9 character accuracy, 9/9 word accuracy
vs BSTD PARSeq  0.449 against 0.73

## What's in the deck

The maths gets three slides, not a footnote. CTC's path-sum formulation and why the blank symbol exists; the feasibility constraint T ≥ |ℓ| + r that *derives* the 48-pixel input height from first principles (a vowel sign adds a codepoint but no width, so narrow Indic words can become mathematically unlabellable — 1.58% of samples at height 32, 0.39% at 48); and why three metrics are needed, since 2% character error becomes 30% word error in agglutinative languages.

Four slides are things that went wrong, because they're the strongest material. The three dataset defects — including Mozhi's test split sharing every book and over a thousand scanned pages with training, contradicting its own paper. The scene-text result where 250k crops were added to close a gap and didn't. The blind test with the renderer-control that rules out measuring my own bug. The limitations table with a measured number per row.

The parameter breakdown is honest about provenance: 1.57M convolutions, 0.79M recurrence, 0.25M head, and the baseline's ~8.3M is flagged as *computed from their architecture* because their paper publishes none.

## One thing to decide before you present

The deck leads with "9 of 9 on word accuracy" — true, and measured on the corpus's own split. But the slide immediately before it shows that split leaks. I've put both protocols side by side rather than picking the flattering one, and the honest framing is in the speaker notes. If your professor pushes on it, that's the answer, and it's a stronger position than having only the higher number.

Edited for length from the Claude Code sessions in which this project was built.
